It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and a month, starting after I received the clinic award.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / physician / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. It turns into this feeling that those imperfections mean I shouldn’t be trusted, like they prove I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make up for or counteract these repetitive thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s a specific thing I do to cancel it out, exactly. Mostly I just get stuck thinking about it and trying to reassure myself, but I don’t know if I’d call it making up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors when these repetitive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t feel driven to do a specific ritual or behavior. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been feeling like I don’t really deserve the trust people put in me. I’m a doctor, and I keep going over small things from work—little clinical imperfections—and treating them like proof that I’m unsafe or dangerous somehow. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts about these clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: Yes. I keep replaying them over and over, even if they were small things. It turns into this feeling that those imperfections mean I shouldn’t be trusted, like they prove I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make up for or counteract these repetitive thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s a specific thing I do to cancel it out, exactly. Mostly I just get stuck thinking about it and trying to reassure myself, but I don’t know if I’d call it making up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors when these repetitive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t feel driven to do a specific ritual or behavior. It’s more that I get caught up in the thoughts and keep turning them over in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your clinical imperfections affecting your daily life or work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I’m still going to clinic and doing the main parts of my job, but I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent administrative work because I just don’t feel reliable with it right now. And outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans. I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy, so I end up pulling back. It’s not like everything has stopped, but it’s starting to interfere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about these clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed anxiety as much when I’m not thinking about it. It’s more like those thoughts are what pull me into feeling tense or uneasy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

So it’s affecting me, but mostly in those areas.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these thoughts about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: They’re pretty distressing. I keep going over small things I did or might have missed, and it feels like they mean something much bigger—that I shouldn’t be trusted, or that I could be dangerous as a doctor. That’s honestly why I’m here.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I’m still going to clinic and doing the main parts of my job, but I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent administrative work because I just don’t feel reliable with it right now. And outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans. I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy, so I end up pulling back. It’s not like everything has stopped, but it’s starting to interfere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about these clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed anxiety as much when I’m not thinking about it. It’s more like those thoughts are what pull me into feeling tense or uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or concerns outside of your work as a doctor that you feel compelled to think about or deal with?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. Most of what I get stuck on is tied to work and how I’m doing clinically. Outside of that, I don’t think there’s a separate thing I feel compelled to keep thinking about or dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Are there other parts of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel like your ability to function is impaired?\n\nPatient: Aside from work and social stuff, I don’t think there’s another big area that’s clearly impaired. The main things are that I’m still showing up to clinic, but I’m less reliable with some of the administrative tasks, and then outside of work I’m pulling back from people because I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. So it’s affecting me, but mostly in those areas.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these thoughts about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: They’re pretty distressing. I keep going over small things I did or might have missed, and it feels like they mean something much bigger—that I shouldn’t be trusted, or that I could be dangerous as a doctor. That’s honestly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or images that are unrelated to your work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed intrusive images or thoughts that come up out of nowhere outside of the work-related worries.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or mental acts that you feel driven to repeat over and over, outside of thinking about your work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of any repeated habits or rituals I feel forced to do outside of the work-related checking or thinking. Nothing like that has stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or urges that are not related to your work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do. I’ve been trying to think of anything like that, but outside of the doctor-related worries, nothing really comes to mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I keep replaying them over and over, even if they were small things. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts about these clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: Yes. I keep replaying them over and over, even if they were small things. It turns into this feeling that those imperfections mean I shouldn’t be trusted, like they prove I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make up for or counteract these repetitive thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s a specific thing I do to cancel it out, exactly. Mostly I just get stuck thinking about it and trying to reassure myself, but I don’t know if I’d call it making up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors when these repetitive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t feel driven to do a specific ritual or behavior. It’s more that I get caught up in the thoughts and keep turning them over in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your clinical imperfections affecting your daily life or work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I’m still going to clinic and doing the main parts of my job, but I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent administrative work because I just don’t feel reliable with it right now. And outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans. I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy, so I end up pulling back. It’s not like everything has stopped, but it’s starting to interfere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about these clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed anxiety as much when I’m not thinking about it. It’s more like those thoughts are what pull me into feeling tense or uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or concerns outside of your work as a doctor that you feel compelled to think about or deal with?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. Most of what I get stuck on is tied to work and how I’m doing clinically. Outside of that, I don’t think there’s a separate thing I feel compelled to keep thinking about or dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Are there other parts of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel like your ability to function is impaired?\n\nPatient: Aside from work and social stuff, I don’t think there’s another big area that’s clearly impaired. The main things are that I’m still showing up to clinic, but I’m less reliable with some of the administrative tasks, and then outside of work I’m pulling back from people because I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. So it’s affecting me, but mostly in those areas.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these thoughts about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: They’re pretty distressing. I keep going over small things I did or might have missed, and it feels like they mean something much bigger—that I shouldn’t be trusted, or that I could be dangerous as a doctor. That’s honestly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or images that are unrelated to your work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed intrusive images or thoughts that come up out of nowhere outside of the work-related worries.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or mental acts that you feel driven to repeat over and over, outside of thinking about your work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of any repeated habits or rituals I feel forced to do outside of the work-related checking or thinking. Nothing like that has stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or urges that are not related to your work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do. I’ve been trying to think of anything like that, but outside of the doctor-related worries, nothing really comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your ability to function is impaired because of these thoughts about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting myself to clinic, but it’s not like I’m functioning normally. I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent administrative stuff because I don’t feel confident I’ll handle it right.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans. I just don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So it’s starting to affect how reliable I am at work, and I feel more disconnected from people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty sleeping or experiencing changes in your appetite due to these thoughts about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear change in my sleep or appetite that I can tie directly to those thoughts. If there is a difference, it hasn’t stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like you're losing control over your thoughts or emotions when you're not at work or thinking about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m losing control. When I’m away from work, I can feel pretty unsettled or tense, but I haven’t noticed my thoughts or emotions feeling completely out of my control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I haven’t noticed intrusive images or thoughts that come up out of nowhere outside of the work-related worries.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or mental acts that you feel driven to repeat over and over, outside of thinking about your work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of any repeated habits or rituals I feel forced to do outside of the work-related checking or thinking. Nothing like that has stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or urges that are not related to your work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do. I’ve been trying to think of anything like that, but outside of the doctor-related worries, nothing really comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your ability to function is impaired because of these thoughts about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting myself to clinic, but it’s not like I’m functioning normally. I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent administrative stuff because I don’t feel confident I’ll handle it right.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans. I just don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So it’s starting to affect how reliable I am at work, and I feel more disconnected from people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty sleeping or experiencing changes in your appetite due to these thoughts about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear change in my sleep or appetite that I can tie directly to those thoughts. If there is a difference, it hasn’t stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like you're losing control over your thoughts or emotions when you're not at work or thinking about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m losing control. When I’m away from work, I can feel pretty unsettled or tense, but I haven’t noticed my thoughts or emotions feeling completely out of my control.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these thoughts about your clinical imperfections are significantly affecting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I’m still getting to clinic and doing the basics, but it’s not feeling normal. I’ve needed help with some administrative things I’d usually handle myself, and I’ve been avoiding social plans because I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So… yeah, it’s affecting how reliable I feel at work and how connected I feel outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your clinical imperfections are a part of yourself or do they feel like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess, but they don’t feel like normal worries. I keep replaying them over and over, even if they were small things. It turns into this feeling that those imperfections mean I shouldn’t be trusted, like they prove I’m dangerous as a doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make up for or counteract these repetitive thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s a specific thing I do to cancel it out, exactly. Mostly I just get stuck thinking about it and trying to reassure myself, but I don’t know if I’d call it making up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors when these repetitive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t feel driven to do a specific ritual or behavior. It’s more that I get caught up in the thoughts and keep turning them over in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your clinical imperfections affecting your daily life or work as a doctor?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I’m still going to clinic and doing the main parts of my job, but I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent administrative work because I just don’t feel reliable with it right now. And outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans. I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy, so I end up pulling back. It’s not like everything has stopped, but it’s starting to interfere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about these clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed anxiety as much when I’m not thinking about it. It’s more like those thoughts are what pull me into feeling tense or uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or concerns outside of your work as a doctor that you feel compelled to think about or deal with?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. Most of what I get stuck on is tied to work and how I’m doing clinically. Outside of that, I don’t think there’s a separate thing I feel compelled to keep thinking about or dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Are there other parts of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel like your ability to function is impaired?\n\nPatient: Aside from work and social stuff, I don’t think there’s another big area that’s clearly impaired. The main things are that I’m still showing up to clinic, but I’m less reliable with some of the administrative tasks, and then outside of work I’m pulling back from people because I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. I can’t think of any repeated habits or rituals I feel forced to do outside of the work-related checking or thinking. I’ve been trying to think of anything like that, but outside of the doctor-related worries, nothing really comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel like your ability to function is impaired because of these thoughts about your clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting myself to clinic, but it’s not like I’m functioning normally.
